Democrats prepare AI safety, data-center bills for new Congress

Category: AI

Democratic lawmakers are preparing legislation for when they take control of Congress in January, addressing electricity costs tied to data centers, third-party testing of advanced artificial intelligence systems and AI companies' liability for harm caused by their products [35][32]. Their election gains put the party in control of Congress as it prepares to regulate an AI infrastructure buildout that has attracted heavy investment [35][4]. The proposals would reverse the approach of the Republican-led Congress, which focused on promoting AI development and did not pass laws restricting the technology [35]. Democrats and advocates say control of congressional committees will allow the party to advance proposals and compel testimony, even as President Donald Trump opposes mandatory federal AI rules [35]. Some Democrats are already pressing for hearings and legislation [43], while House lawmakers are preparing new AI safety bill drafts for release by year-end. Rep. Don Beyer, a Virginia Democrat, said he hopes a bill will be ready for the House floor in the first quarter of 2027 [49].

Primary Source: View original article

My great hope is that at least in the first quarter we have put together a good bill that is ready to go to the House floor.

— Rep. Don Beyer, Virginia Democrat (Bloomberg Government)

Did you know: Data Center Watch found that 32 of 34 winning gubernatorial candidates in races called by The Associated Press supported stronger restrictions or regulation, and 12 supported bans or moratoriums [99].

Key Points

  • Policy limits: Although Democrats take control of both chambers in January, President Trump can veto federal legislation. Analysts cited by CBS News expect opposition to data centers to have more impact at state and local levels [158].
  • Spending scale: Capital Economics estimates that Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Microsoft will spend about $800 billion on data centers and related infrastructure in 2026, rising to $1 trillion in 2027 [152].
  • Texas capacity: Texas is expected to account for 25% to 30% of U.S. data-center capacity coming online by 2030; the state has 169 operational facilities, 118 under construction and more than 500 planned [85].
  • Local pushback: Texas agriculture commissioner-elect Clayton Tucker said ranchers concerned about dwindling water supplies made opposition to data-center development central to his campaign [25].
  • Local ballots: Voters approved restrictions or rejected pro-development proposals in at least 44 of 47 local data-center measures, while an NBC Election Day poll found 76% opposed facilities in their communities [85][111].
  • Campaign spending: Candidates spent more than $380 million on television ads mentioning data centers, and Democrats outspent Republicans on the topic by more than 3 to 1, according to AdImpact data [25].
  • Company orders: Hyosung Heavy Industries reported a record 18.9 trillion-won heavy-industry backlog in the third quarter. HD Hyundai Electric had an $8.84 billion order backlog, including a 1.12 trillion-won supply agreement, while LS Electric reported a 7.04 trillion-won backlog [165].
  • Ballot opposition: Of 41 data-center-related ballot measures in the midterms, voters in all but one backed additional restrictions, TechCrunch reported [36].
  • Texas pipeline: Texas has 169 operating data centers, 118 under construction and more than 500 planned. The state is expected to account for 25% to 30% of U.S. capacity scheduled to come online by 2030 [109].
  • Lawmakers’ agenda: Proposals discussed by Democrats include protections against data centers raising electricity costs, independent testing of advanced AI systems and clearer company liability for harm caused by AI products [110].
  • OpenAI commitments: OpenAI has pledged to cover the full cost of electricity and infrastructure upgrades for its data centers, favor cooling systems that use less water and publish its utility-use information [17].
  • Funding plans: Friar said OpenAI had closed an initial $11 billion portion of a funding round and could raise as much as $9 billion more; she also said the company’s business grew 70% quarter over quarter in the third quarter [33][72].

Perspectives

President Donald Trump: He said he would work with states that want to become rich by embracing AI [35].

Sources: Reuters

OpenAI: OpenAI President Greg Brockman said the company must better explain to local communities why data centers can benefit them; the company also urged lawmakers to put its commitments on electricity costs and water use into law [17].

Sources: KRCG

Common Sense Media: Founder and CEO Jim Steyer said voters want stronger AI safeguards, while Chief Advocacy Officer Danny Weiss urged Congress to revisit proposals on AI companions for children and legal liability for AI systems [43].

Sources: The National News Desk

OpenAI: The company’s published commitments include giving residents access to construction plans and limiting nondisclosure agreements to narrow protections for security information, trade secrets or sensitive proprietary information [17][111].

Sources: KRCG-TV

Data Center Coalition: Coalition official Dan Diorio warned that moratoriums could mean host communities lose tax revenue or payments from developers, as well as investment associated with data-center construction [99].

Sources: ABC News

Bernie Sanders, independent U.S. senator from Vermont: Sanders called for a nationwide data-center moratorium, arguing that large technology companies threaten the environment, jobs and humanity’s future [17].

Sources: KRCG-TV

OpenAI: The company says it will stop using nondisclosure agreements with public officials in site discussions and give residents access to construction plans and information about their potential effects [112].

Sources: CNBC

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett: Hassett argued that ballot initiatives would not affect data centers already scheduled for the next few years and said construction could continue in states with more welcoming governors [114].

Sources: CNBC

Texas Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa: Hinojosa has called for pausing new data-center construction in Texas until protections for residents are in place, saying rural communities lack rules to protect Texans [109].

Sources: CBS News

Timeline

  • 3 November 2026: Voters considered 41 data-center measures during midterm elections [36]
  • 6 November 2026: Democrats outlined plans for AI and data-center legislation [35]
  • By year-end 2026: Beyer and Jacobs plan to release an initial safety bill draft [49]
  • January 2027: Democrats take control of Congress and plan to introduce bills [35]
  • July 2025: President Trump signed an executive order to accelerate data-center development [107]
  • 3 November 2026: Voters backed restrictions and candidates promising tighter data-center oversight [85][96]
  • 5 November 2026: OpenAI announced community commitments, including energy and water disclosures [17][112]
  • 6 November 2026: OpenAI CFO Sarah Friar reaffirmed expansion and fundraising plans [33]
  • 3 November 2026: Midterm elections returned data-center critics to office [109]
  • 5 November 2026: OpenAI published its data-center community commitments [157][33]
  • 6 November 2026: CFO Sarah Friar said OpenAI would keep investing [33]

Industry Impact

  • Semiconductors: A slowdown in new data centers could weigh on demand expectations for suppliers including Nvidia, Micron and SK Hynix, while hyperscalers’ use of both GPUs and in-house accelerator chips supports a different supply-chain mix [117].
  • AI cloud providers: GPU-focused providers such as CoreWeave and Nebius may be especially exposed to delayed facilities because their growth depends on rapidly expanding rented GPU capacity [117].
  • Electric utilities and communities: Wisconsin Gov.-elect David Crowley is considering requiring developers to disclose water and energy use, demonstrate community support for projects and shield ratepayers and taxpayers from energy costs [99].
  • Power equipment: South Korean suppliers HD Hyundai Electric, Hyosung Heavy Industries and LS Electric have a combined 37.9 trillion-won backlog that could be affected by U.S. project delays. North America accounts for 60% of Hyosung’s heavy-industry backlog [165].

Business Angle

  • AI-related data-center, power and equipment spending represented about 20% of U.S. GDP over the preceding year [152].
  • Friar said OpenAI would continue to consider a public offering in 2027 [33].
  • Anthropic: Prospective investors in its initial public offering are questioning whether its roadshow can proceed on schedule and pushing for a lower valuation amid market turmoil [112].
  • Copper fell 2.7% this week as investors priced in the possibility of weaker data-center demand [120].

International Reactions

  • 🇹🇼 Taiwan: The election-driven selloff hit Taiwan sharply: its benchmark index fell 1,873 points on Nov. 6 after an intraday drop of 3,738 points, while TSMC briefly touched its daily limit [117].
  • 🇦🇺 Australia: Researchers writing about Australia said U.S. opposition could redirect investment toward jurisdictions with fewer restrictions, while Australia is preparing national standards for large data centers covering energy, water, siting and community engagement [107].

Q&A

Q: What is the likely source of new rules if Congress does not pass a national standard?

A: State and local governments could impose different siting, disclosure and utility-cost requirements. Political strategist Aryan Ranjan told TechCrunch that the AI rules companies face over the next two years may depend more on where they operate than on federal action [36].

Q: When could tougher local approval rules begin to affect new projects?

A: A U.S. economist quoted by Dutch broadcaster NOS said projects scheduled before 2027 are unlikely to be reversed quickly because construction has often begun and permits are in place. New applications from 2028 could face tougher approvals [160].
